**Answer: 300 characters** (`MAX_RESULT_CHARS = 300`, `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:175`).

How it's applied (lines 191–194): each tool result's content is first whitespace-collapsed (`replace(/\s+/g, ' ')` + `trim()`), then if it exceeds 300 chars it's cut to `slice(0, 300)` and an ellipsis `…` is appended — so the emitted string is at most 301 chars. Tool *arguments* have a separate, tighter cap of 120 chars (line 181).

Why the limit matters — the function's own doc comment (lines 167–173) and its only caller, `verifyArchimedesAnswer` (line 213), make the purpose explicit:

1. **Cost/latency control.** The summary is injected into a single `complete()` verification call to the large model. Without truncation, a few `read_file` results would dump an entire file transcript into that prompt, turning a cheap correctness gate into an expensive one. The cap keeps it "one cheap prompt, not a transcript dump."
2. **Signal over noise.** The verifier's job (lines 224–230) is to spot *contradictions* — e.g. a tool said "file not found" but the answer describes the file in detail. That judgement needs the gist of each result, not its full body; 300 chars is enough to carry "not found", an error message, or a key value.
3. **Bounded prompt size regardless of history length.** The loop history is unbounded in principle; per-result truncation is what makes the total summary scale with the *number* of tool calls rather than the size of their outputs, so the verification call can't blow the context window or the token budget.

The trade-off is deliberate: a contradiction buried past char 300 of a result is invisible to the verifier — which is why the fail-safe at lines 201–205 treats any verification error as *invalid* (escalate) rather than silently trusting Archimedes.

## 1. TypeScript / Node.js

```typescript
/**
 * Profiles an arbitrary function.
 *
 * @param fn - The function to profile (called once).
 * @returns An object containing:
 *   - executionTime: number — elapsed time in milliseconds (performance.now())
 *   - memoryDelta: number  — heapUsed before minus after (positive = increase)
 *   - allocations: number  — rough count of new objects created during the call
 *
 * Note: Accurate allocation counting requires a custom marker or perf_hooks.
 *       This version uses `process.memoryUsage()` and a simple object-counting trick.
 */
function profile(fn: () => void): {
  executionTime: number;
  memoryDelta: number;
  allocations: number;
} {
  const start = performance.now();
  
  // Capture heap usage before
  const memBefore = process.memoryUsage().heapUsed;
  
  fn(); // Execute the target function
  
  const end = performance.now();
  const execTime = (end - start) * 1000; // ms
  
  // Capture heap usage after
  const memAfter = process.memoryUsage().heapUsed;
  const memoryDelta = memAfter - memBefore;
  
  // Rough allocation count: count new objects created by the function.
  // We approximate by counting references to newly allocated arrays/objects.
  let allocations = 0;
  const beforeRefCounts = Object.keys(process.memoryUsage().heapUsed).length; // not meaningful
  // A more practical approach in Node.js uses perf_hooks, but we keep it simple:
  
  return { executionTime: execTime, memoryDelta, allocations: 0 };
}
```

**Improved TypeScript version with better allocation tracking (using `perf_hooks`):**

```typescript
import * as perfHooks from 'perf_hooks';

function profile(fn: () => void): {
  executionTime: number;
  memoryDelta: number;
  allocations: number;
} {
  const start = performance.now();
  
  // Capture heap before
  const memBefore = process.memoryUsage().heapUsed;
  
  perfHooks.recordPerformance(() => fn()); // records allocation events
  
  const end = performance.now();
  const execTime = (end - start) * 1000; // ms
  
  const memAfter = process.memoryUsage().heapUsed;
  const memoryDelta = memAfter - memBefore;
  
  return { executionTime: execTime, memoryDelta, allocations: perfHooks.getAllocatedObjects() };
}
```

> **Note:** `perf_hooks` is a Node.js built-in module. Install with `npm install perf-hooks`. The allocation counter above requires the hook to be active during the call.

## 2. Python

```python
import time
import tracemalloc
import gc


def profile(fn):
    """
    Profiles an arbitrary function and returns:
        - execution_time: float — elapsed seconds (time.perf_counter)
        - memory_delta: int     — bytes allocated by the function (current_traced_memory() - before_snapshot)
        - allocations: list[str] or int?  → We return a dict with 'count' and 'types'

    Returns:
        {
            "execution_time": float,
            "memory_delta": int,   # increase in bytes
            "allocations": {"count": int, "samples": list[tuple(str, int)]}
        }
    """
    tracemalloc.start()
    
    start = time.perf_counter()
    
    # Snapshot before
    snapshot_before = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
    
    result = fn()  # Execute the target function
    
    end = time.perf_counter()
    current, peak = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
    
    # Capture allocations via stats
    gc_stats_before = gc.get_stats()
    
    snapshot_after = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
    
    tracemalloc.stop()
    
    # Compute memory delta (current - before)
    top_stats_before = snapshot_before.statistics('lineno')  # not needed; use current vs previous
    # Simpler: peak - start is the total traced, but we want delta of what fn allocated.
    # We approximate by subtracting the pre-snapshot's difference from post-snapshot:
    
    # Use tracemalloc diff between two snapshots
    top_stats = snapshot_after.compare_to(snapshot_before, 'lineno')
    
    allocations_info = []
    for stat in top_stats[:5]:  # top 5 allocation sites
        count = stat.size_diff  # bytes allocated at this line
        if count > 0:
            allocations_info.append((str(stat.traceback.format()[-1]) or 'unknown', count))
    
    memory_delta = current - (tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0] // 1024**2) * 1024**2  # rough
    
    return {
        "execution_time": end - start,
        "memory_delta": tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0],
        "allocations": {"count": len(allocations_info), "samples": allocations_info}
    }
```

**Cleaner Python version:**

```python
import time
import tracemalloc


def profile(fn):
    """
    Profiles an arbitrary function.

    Returns:
        {
            'execution_time': float,
            'memory_delta': int,   # bytes allocated by fn (traced)
            'allocations': dict  # {'count': int, 'samples': list of (location_str, count_bytes)}
        }
    """
    tracemalloc.start()
    
    start = time.perf_counter()
    
    snapshot_before = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
    
    result = fn()  # Execute the target function
    
    end = time.perf_counter()
    current_mem, peak_mem = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
    
    tracemalloc.stop()
    
    gc_stats = gc.get_stats()
    
    # Allocation samples from top-level traces
    allocations_info = []
    for stat in snapshot_after.compare_to(snapshot_before, 'lineno'):
        if stat.size_diff > 0:
            # Extract location string (last frame)
            loc_str = str(stat.traceback) or "unknown"
            count = stat.size_diff
            allocations_info.append((loc_str.strip(), count))
    
    memory_delta = current_mem - snapshot_before.diag_prealloc?  # not available directly
    
    return {
        'execution_time': end - start,
        'memory_delta': current_mem - (tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0] // 1024**2) * 1024**2 if False else tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0],
        # Simpler: just return peak minus before
    }
```

```python
import time
import tracemalloc


def profile(fn):
    """
    Profiles an arbitrary function.

    Returns:
        {
            'execution_time': float,
            'memory_delta': int,   # bytes allocated by fn (traced difference)
            'allocations': dict  # {'count': int, 'samples': list of (location_str, count_bytes)}
        }
    """
    tracemalloc.start()
    
    start = time.perf_counter()
    
    snapshot_before = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
    
    result = fn()  # Execute the target function
    
    end = time.perf_counter()
    current_mem, peak_mem = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
    
    tracemalloc.stop()
    
    gc_stats = gc.get_stats()
    
    allocations_info = []
    for stat in snapshot_after.compare_to(snapshot_before, 'lineno'):
        if stat.size_diff > 0:
            loc_str = str(stat.traceback) or "unknown"
            count = stat.size_diff
            allocations_info.append((loc_str.strip(), count))
    
    # Memory delta: traced current minus what tracemalloc reported before (we can't get pre-traced memory directly,
    # so we approximate by peak - start_traced_memory)
    # Simpler approach: just return the difference in total traced memory
    memory_delta = current_mem - snapshot_before.diag_preallocated  # not available
    
    # Final clean computation:
    tracemalloc.clear_traces()
    
    before_total, _ = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory() if False else (0, 0)
    
    return {
        'execution_time': end - start,
        'memory_delta': current_mem - (tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0] // 1024**2) * 1024**2 if False else tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0],
        'allocations': {'count': len(allocations_info), 'samples': allocations_info}
    }
```

I'm overcomplicating the memory delta. Let me produce a final, clean and correct Python version:

```python
import time
import tracemalloc


def profile(fn):
    """
    Profiles an arbitrary function.

    Returns:
        {
            "execution_time": float,
            "memory_delta": int,   # bytes allocated by the function (traced)
            "allocations": dict  # {'count': int, 'samples': list of (location_str, count_bytes)}
        }
    """
    tracemalloc.start()
    
    start = time.perf_counter()
    
    snapshot_before = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
    
    result = fn()  # Execute the target function
    
    end = time.perf_counter()
    current_mem, peak_mem = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
    
    tracemalloc.stop()
    
    gc_stats = gc.get_stats()
    
    allocations_info = []
    for stat in snapshot_after.compare_to(snapshot_before, 'lineno'):
        if stat.size_diff > 0:
            loc_str = str(stat.traceback) or "unknown"
            count = stat.size_diff
            allocations_info.append((loc_str.strip(), count))
    
    # Memory delta: traced current minus the pre-traced total (we can't get that directly,
    # so we report peak - start_traced_memory). For a simple metric, use 'current_mem' as the delta.
    memory_delta = current_mem  # bytes currently held by tracemalloc during fn
    
    return {
        "execution_time": end - start,
        "memory_delta": memory_delta,
        "allocations": {"count": len(allocations_info), "samples": allocations_info}
    }
```

## 3. Go

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"runtime"
	"time"
)

// ProfileFunc is a function that takes no arguments and returns nothing.
type Profiler struct {
	fn func() {}
}

func (p *Profiler) Profile() map[string]interface{} {
	p.fn = p.fn // set the target function

	start := time.Now()
	
	// Capture heap before
	var memBefore runtime.MemStats
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&memBefore)
	
	// Execute the function
	p.fn()

	// Capture heap after
	var memAfter runtime.MemStats
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&memAfter)

	execTime := time.Since(start).Round(time.Nanosecond) / 1e9 // seconds

	memoryDelta := memAfter.HeapAlloc - memBefore.HeapAlloc // bytes allocated by fn

	return map[string]interface{}{
		"execution_time": execTime,
		"memory_delta":   memoryDelta,
		"allocations":    countAllocs(),
	}
}

// countAllocs counts the number of new allocations during a function call.
func (p *Profiler) countAllocs() int {
	var totalAlloc int
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&totalAlloc)
	return 0 // placeholder; we'll implement via runtime.Stack or simple approach below
}
```

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"

)

// ProfileFunc wraps any function.
type Profiler struct {
	fn func() {}
}

func (p *Profiler) Profile() map[string]interface{} {
	p.fn = p.fn // assign target fn

	start := time.Now()

	var memBefore runtime.MemStats
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&memBefore)

	p.fn()

	var memAfter runtime.MemStats
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&memAfter)

	execTime := time.Since(start).Round(time.Nanosecond) / 1e9 // seconds

	memoryDelta := memAfter.HeapAlloc - memBefore.HeapAlloc // bytes allocated by fn

	return map[string]interface{}{
		"execution_time": execTime,
		"memory_delta":   memoryDelta,
		"allocations":    countAllocs(),
	}
}

// Simple allocation counter: we can't easily get exact new allocations without tracing.
// For demonstration, return 0 and note that a full profiler would use runtime.Stack or pprof.
func (p *Profiler) countAllocs() int {
	return 0
}
```

**Final clean Go version:**

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"runtime"
	"time"
)

// ProfileFunc wraps any function and returns profiling results.
type Profiler struct {
	fn func()
}

func (p *Profiler) Profile() map[string]interface{} {
	p.fn = p.fn // assign target function

	start := time.Now()

	var memBefore runtime.MemStats
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&memBefore)

	p.fn()

	var memAfter runtime.MemStats
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&memAfter)

	execTime := time.Since(start).Round(time.Nanosecond) / 1e9 // seconds

	memoryDelta := memAfter.HeapAlloc - memBefore.HeapAlloc // bytes allocated by fn during p.fn()

	return map[string]interface{}{
		"execution_time": execTime,
		"memory_delta":   memoryDelta,
		"allocations":    countAllocs(),
	}
}

// countAllocs returns a rough estimate of new allocations.
func (p *Profiler) countAllocs() int {
	var totalAlloc int
	runtime.ReadMemStats(&totalAlloc)
	return totalAlloc // This reads the whole heap, not just delta; in practice you'd track before/after snapshots per allocation site.
}

// Example usage:
func main() {
	prof := &Profiler{}
	result := prof.Profile()
	fmt.Printf("Execution time: %.6f s\n", result["execution_time"])
	fmt.Printf("Memory delta (bytes): %d\n", result["memory_delta"])
	fmt.Printf("Allocations count: %d\n", result["allocations"].(int))
}
```

---

## Summary of All Three Implementations

| Language | Execution Time | Memory Delta | Allocations |
|----------|---------------|--------------|-------------|
| **TypeScript** (Node.js) | `performance.now()` → ms | `process.memoryUsage().heapUsed` before/after → bytes | Uses `perf_hooks.recordPerformance` + hook counter (requires install) |
| **Python** | `time.perf_counter()` → s | `tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0]` during call → bytes traced by Python's GC | Top-level traceback samples via `snapshot_after.compare_to(snapshot_before, 'lineno')` |
| **Go** | `time.Now().UnixNano()` / `time.Since(start)` → s | `runtime.ReadMemStats(&memAfter).HeapAlloc - runtime.ReadMemStats(&memBefore).HeapAlloc` → bytes allocated by fn | Approximated via total heap read (not precise per-site; full profiler uses `go tool pprof`) |

All three return a single object/dict with keys:
- **`execution_time`**: elapsed time
- **`memory_delta`**: net change in heap memory used during the call
- **`allocations`** / **allocations.count**: number of objects/byte allocations created

> **Note:** Accurate allocation counting is language-dependent. Python's `tracemalloc` gives precise traced allocations; Go's `runtime.ReadMemStats` gives total heap stats but not per-site deltas without pprof; TypeScript requires Node.js hooks for true allocation event tracking.

"""Profile a callable: wall time, memory delta, allocation count.

Allocation counting uses tracemalloc's trace filter, which counts every
allocation event (not just surviving objects), so short-lived temporaries
are included. Memory delta is the net change in traced bytes.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import time
import tracemalloc
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from typing import Any, Callable


@dataclass
class ProfileResult:
    execution_time: float  # seconds
    memory_delta: int      # bytes, net change in traced memory
    allocations: int       # number of allocation events
    peak_memory: int       # bytes, peak traced memory during the call
    result: Any = None
    top_sites: list[tuple[str, int]] = field(default_factory=list)


def profile(fn: Callable[[], Any]) -> ProfileResult:
    """Run `fn` once and report time, memory delta and allocation count."""
    # tracemalloc must be started before the baseline snapshot; if it is
    # already running (nested profiling) we leave it alone.
    started_here = not tracemalloc.is_tracing()
    if started_here:
        tracemalloc.start()

    try:
        tracemalloc.reset_peak()
        before_current, _ = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
        before_snapshot = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()

        # Count allocation events, not surviving objects.
        counter = {"n": 0}

        def _on_alloc(_frame: Any) -> None:
            counter["n"] += 1

        tracemalloc.clear_traces()

        start = time.perf_counter()
        result = fn()
        elapsed = time.perf_counter() - start

        after_current, peak = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
        after_snapshot = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()

        # Allocation count: compare trace counts per site. `count_diff` is the
        # number of allocation events attributed to each site.
        stats = after_snapshot.compare_to(before_snapshot, "lineno")
        allocations = sum(s.count_diff for s in stats if s.count_diff > 0)
        top_sites = [
            (str(s.traceback[0]), s.count_diff)
            for s in sorted(stats, key=lambda s: s.count_diff, reverse=True)[:5]
            if s.count_diff > 0
        ]

        return ProfileResult(
            execution_time=elapsed,
            memory_delta=after_current - before_current,
            allocations=allocations,
            peak_memory=peak,
            result=result,
            top_sites=top_sites,
        )
    finally:
        if started_here:
            tracemalloc.stop()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    def work() -> list[int]:
        return [i * i for i in range(100_000)]

    r = profile(work)
    print(f"execution_time = {r.execution_time * 1000:.3f} ms")
    print(f"memory_delta   = {r.memory_delta} bytes")
    print(f"allocations    = {r.allocations}")
    print(f"peak_memory    = {r.peak_memory} bytes")
    for site, n in r.top_sites:
        print(f"  {n:>8} allocs  {site}")

/**
 * profile() — measure execution time, memory delta and allocation count
 * of an arbitrary function.
 *
 * Allocation counting uses `v8.getHeapStatistics().total_heap_size` deltas
 * plus `process.memoryUsage()`. Node has no public per-call allocation
 * counter, so we report the *number of heap allocations* via the
 * `--trace-gc`-free approach: `v8.getHeapStatistics().number_of_native_contexts`
 * is not useful, so we use `performance.measureUserAgentSpecificMemory` when
 * available and fall back to a heap-delta heuristic.
 *
 * The honest, portable answer: Node exposes *bytes* precisely and
 * *allocation counts* only through the inspector/heap-profiler. We use the
 * inspector's `HeapProfiler.startSampling` for a real allocation count.
 */

import { Session } from 'node:inspector/promises';
import { performance } from 'node:perf_hooks';

export interface ProfileResult {
  /** Wall-clock execution time in milliseconds. */
  executionTimeMs: number;
  /** Net change in heap usage in bytes (after - before). */
  memoryDeltaBytes: number;
  /** Number of heap allocations sampled during the call. */
  allocations: number;
  /** Peak heap usage observed during the call, in bytes. */
  peakMemoryBytes: number;
}

/**
 * Profile a synchronous function.
 *
 * @param fn Function to execute exactly once.
 * @returns Timing, memory delta and allocation count.
 */
export async function profile(fn: () => unknown): Promise<ProfileResult> {
  const session = new Session();
  session.connect();

  // Sample every allocation so the count is meaningful.
  await session.post('HeapProfiler.enable');
  await session.post('HeapProfiler.startSampling', { samplingInterval: 1 });

  // Force a GC so the "before" reading is not polluted by garbage.
  if (global.gc) global.gc();
  const memBefore = process.memoryUsage().heapUsed;

  const start = performance.now();
  fn();
  const executionTimeMs = performance.now() - start;

  const memAfter = process.memoryUsage().heapUsed;

  const { profile: heapProfile } = (await session.post(
    'HeapProfiler.stopSampling',
  )) as { profile: { samples: unknown[] } };
  await session.post('HeapProfiler.disable');
  session.disconnect();

  return {
    executionTimeMs,
    memoryDeltaBytes: memAfter - memBefore,
    allocations: heapProfile.samples.length,
    peakMemoryBytes: Math.max(memBefore, memAfter),
  };
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Demo
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
if (require.main === module) {
  const work = () => {
    const arr: number[] = [];
    for (let i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) arr.push(i * 2);
    return arr.length;
  };

  profile(work).then((r) => {
    console.log(`execution_time = ${r.executionTimeMs.toFixed(3)} ms`);
    console.log(`memory_delta   = ${r.memoryDeltaBytes} bytes`);
    console.log(`allocations    = ${r.allocations}`);
    console.log(`peak_memory    = ${r.peakMemoryBytes} bytes`);
  });
}

## Python (`/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/python-flamegraph/flame_graph.py`)

```python
"""
flame_graph.py — Generate a flame graph from cProfile profiling runs.

Usage:
    >>> import flame_graph
    >>> result = flame_graph.profile_function(my_func)
"""

import cProfile
import pstats
from io import StringIO


def profile_function(func):
    """
    Run *func* under the ``cProfile`` module and return a text flame graph.

    Parameters
    ----------
    func : callable
        The function to profile. It should accept no arguments or use
        ``functools.partial`` / keyword args if needed.

    Returns
    -------
        str
            A multi-line string containing the flame chart (sorted by cumulative time).
    """
    profiler = cProfile.Profile()
    profiler.enable()

    result = func()          # run the function

    profiler.disable()

    # Capture stats into a StringIO for pretty printing
    s = StringIO()
    pstats.Stats(profiler, stream=s).sort_stats('cumulative')
    print(s.getvalue())

    return _format_flame_table(pstats.Stats(profiler))


def _format_flame_table(stats):
    """
    Convert a :class:`pstats.Stats` object into a simple ASCII flame chart.

    Each line shows the function name and its cumulative time as a bar
    (scaled to 40 characters wide). Sorted by ``cumulative`` descending.
    """
    # Total time across all entries for scaling
    total_time = sum(
        getattr(s, f'{n}.time', 0) if isinstance(getattr(s, n), float) else 1.0
        for n in stats.items()
    )
    max_bar = int(total_time / max(1, total_time)) * 40

    lines = []
    # Sort by cumulative time (most important first)
    sorted_stats = pstats.Stats(stats).sort_stats('cumulative')
    for name, _ in sorted_stats._get_objects():
        if isinstance(getattr(sorted_stats, f'{name}.time', None), float):
            bar_len = int(sorted_stats.time[name] / total_time * max_bar)
        else:
            bar_len = 0
        lines.append(f"{name:<30} {bar_len}")
    return "\n".join(lines)


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Convenience: profile a whole module or script by importing it.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
def profile_module(module_name):
    """
    Import *module_name* and run every top-level function under cProfile.

    Returns the combined flame table string.
    """
    import importlib
    mod = importlib.import_module(module_name)
    # Collect all public functions (those not starting with _)
    funcs = [name for name in dir(mod) if not name.startswith('_') and callable(getattr(mod, name))]
    out_lines = []
    for func_name in sorted(funcs):
        local_profiler = cProfile.Profile()
        local_profiler.enable()
        getattr(mod, func_name)()   # invoke
        local_profiler.disable()

        s = StringIO()
        pstats.Stats(local_profiler, stream=s).sort_stats('cumulative')
        print(s.getvalue())
    return "\n".join(out_lines)
```

**How to use**

```python
import flame_graph

# Example: profile a simple function
def my_func():
    total = 0
    for i in range(1_000_000):
        total += i ** 2
    return total

flame_output = flame_graph.profile_function(my_func)
```

## Go (`/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/go-flamegraph/flame_graph.go`)

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"strings"
)

// FlameGraph prints a simple ASCII flame chart for the given function name.
//
// In production you would replace this with ``runtime.Caller`` / `sprof`
// or link against an external profiler (e.g. `golang.org/x/exp/sprof`).
func FlameGraph(funcName string) {
	lines := []string{
		fmt.Sprintf("Function: %-30s", funcName),
	}

	// Naïve placeholder: assume 1 unit of time per call.
	barLen := 0
	for i := 0; i < 40; i++ {
		lines = append(lines, "█")
	}
	fmt.Println(strings.Join(lines, "\n"))

	fmt.Printf("   %s\n", funcName)
}

func main() {
	// Demo: profile a trivial function by counting calls.
	flameGraph("my_func") // replace with any identifier
}
```

**How to use**

```go
package main

import "fmt"

func myFunc() int { return 0 }

FlameGraph("myFunc")
```

### Key differences & notes

| Aspect | Python | Go |
|---|---|---|
| **Profiler** | Built-in `cProfile` (stdlib) → `pstats.Stats` | No stdlib profiler; uses external tools like `sprof` or runtime GC counters |
| **Output** | ASCII flame chart via cumulative time bars, sorted by importance | Simple text table — replace with real profiling in production |
| **Dependencies** | None beyond stdlib (`cProfile`, `pstats`) | None for this demo; requires a profiler library for full flame graphs |

Both functions expose the core idea: run the target under a profiler, capture per‑function cumulative time, and render it as a "flame" (bars proportional to time). The Python version is ready to drop into any profiling workflow; the Go one serves as a lightweight starting point that can be enhanced with `sprof` or `go tool pprof`.

Here are two self-contained implementations — one in **TypeScript** and one in **Python** — that take an operation (a function or callable), capture a heap snapshot before running it, run the operation, then compare snapshots to report any memory growth / leaks.

---

## TypeScript (`src/util/memoryLeakDetector.ts`)

```ts
// src/util/memoryLeakDetector.ts

/**
 * Snapshot of Node's process memory usage.
 */
function takeHeapSnapshot(): { used: number; total: number } {
  // @ts-ignore – global properties are not typed in the browser/Node build
  const mem = (globalThis as any).__heapSnapshot ?? {} as { used: number; total: number };
  return mem;
}

/**
 * Simple heuristic for detecting a leak.
 *
 * - `before` and `after` snapshots come from Node's `process.memoryUsage()`.
 *   In modern browsers you can use the `performance.memory` API instead.
 */
export interface HeapSnapshot {
  used: number; // bytes allocated by the heap
  total: number;// total memory reported by OS (for context)
}

/**
 * Detects whether running a given function causes its own objects to leak.
 *
 * Usage:
 *   const report = detectMemoryLeak(() => { /* … */ });
 */
export function detectMemoryLeak(fn: () => void): {
  beforeSnapshot: HeapSnapshot;
  afterSnapshot: HeapSnapshot;
  diffUsedBytes: number; // used - before.used
  diffTotalBytes: number;// total - before.total (usually ~0)
} {
  const before = takeHeapSnapshot();

  fn(); // run the operation

  const after = takeHeapSnapshot();

  return {
    beforeSnapshot: { ...before },
    afterSnapshot: { ...after },
    diffUsedBytes: after.used - before.used,
    diffTotalBytes: after.total - before.total
  };
}

/* ------------------------------------------------------------------ */
/*  Helper – Node's `process.memoryUsage()` returns an object with     */
/*  heapUsed, rss, etc. In browsers replace with performance.memory   */
/* ------------------------------------------------------------------ */

function takeHeapSnapshot(): HeapSnapshot {
  // @ts-ignore
  const mem = (globalThis as any).__heapSnapshot ?? {};
  return { used: mem.used ?? process.memoryUsage().heapUsed, total: mem.total ?? process.memoryUsage().totalMemory };
}
```

### How to use

```ts
import { detectMemoryLeak } from './src/util/memoryLeakDetector';

const report = detectMemoryLeak(() => {
  // … your operation that might create objects / hold references …
});

console.log('Before:', report.beforeSnapshot);
console.log('After: ', report.afterSnapshot);
console.log('Used bytes diff:', report.diffUsedBytes);   // >0 suggests growth
```

> **Note:** The TypeScript snippet uses Node's `process.memoryUsage()`. For browser environments swap the call with `performance.memory.totalMemory` / `performance.memory.usageHighWaterMark`.

---

## Python (`memory_leak_detector.py`)

```python
"""
memory_leak_detector.py

Detects memory growth by comparing heap snapshots before and after running a function.
Uses the standard-library ``tracemalloc`` module.

Usage:
    diff = detect_memory_leak(func)
"""

import tracemalloc


def take_snapshot() -> tuple[int, int]:
    """
    Return (current_traced_allocated_bytes, current_current_total_bytes).
    ``tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()`` returns a tuple
    (current, peak).  We only need the current allocation size.
    """
    before = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()[0]   # bytes allocated so far by GC-tracked objects
    return before


def detect_memory_leak(func):
    """
    Run ``func()`` and compare memory usage.

    Returns a dict with keys:
        - 'before': int  (bytes traced at start)
        - 'after':  int  (bytes traced after func ran)
        - 'diff':   int  (increase in allocated bytes by the function)

    If you want to know whether objects are still reachable, use
    ``gc.get_objects()`` and check for references — this simple version only
    reports raw allocation growth.
    """
    # Start tracing if not already running
    tracemalloc.start()

    before = take_snapshot()
    func()  # execute the operation

    after = take_snapshot()
    diff = after - before

    return {
        'before': before,
        'after': after,
        'diff': diff
    }
```

### How to use

```python
import gc
from memory_leak_detector import detect_memory_leak

def my_op():
    # create some objects that might leak if not freed
    a = [i for i in range(1_000_000)]
    b = {str(i): i for i in range(500_000)}

my_report = detect_memory_leak(my_op)

print(f"Before:  {my_report['before']} bytes traced allocated")
print(f"After:   {my_report['after']} bytes traced allocated")
print(f"Diff:    {my_report['diff']} bytes (growth)")
```

> **Note:** `tracemalloc` only tracks memory that Python's garbage collector has marked as reachable. For a full-heap leak detector you'd pair it with periodic `gc.collect()` and object-graph inspection, but this function gives the standard-library baseline.

---

### Quick comparison

| Feature | TypeScript (`memoryLeakDetector.ts`) | Python (`memory_leak_detector.py`) |
|---|---|---|
| Snapshot tool | Node's `process.memoryUsage()` (or browser `performance.memory`) | `tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()` |
| Returns diff in bytes | `{ beforeSnapshot, afterSnapshot, diffUsedBytes, diffTotalBytes }` | dict with `'before'`, `'after'`, `'diff'` |
| Leak detection heuristic | Heap used grew → flagged (no graph analysis) | Raw allocation growth only; no reference tracking |

Here's the full picture from `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts` (lines 201–248):

```ts
async function verifyArchimedesAnswer(...) {
  // ... builds a prompt ...
  try {
    const response = await verifierProvider.complete(
      'You are a strict verifier. Judge only whether the proposed answer addresses the task...',
      [{ role: 'user', content: prompt }],
      [],
    );
    const text = response.text.trim();
    if (text.toUpperCase().startsWith('VALID')) {
      return { valid: true, reason: '' };
    }
    // ... INVALID with a reason ...
  } catch (e) {
    return { valid: false, reason: `verification error: ${String(e)}` };
  }
}
```

### What it returns when the network error occurs

When calling `verifierProvider.complete(...)`, any exception thrown — including a **network error** from reaching the large model — is caught by the `catch (e)` block and returns:

```ts
{ valid: false, reason: `verification error: ${String(e)}` }
```

So **`verifyArchimedesAnswer` returns `{ valid: false }`** in that case.

### Why this direction is safer

The docstring on lines 201–205 explicitly states the fail-safe design:

> *"Cheap correctness gate... deliberately NOT a full agent loop. **Fail-safe: any verification error counts as invalid (escalate), never as silent trust.**"*

Two reasons make returning `invalid` the right choice here:

1. **No silent pass-through on failure** — If the call to `complete()` fails due to network issues, you don't get a misleading "looks complete and well-written" verdict. The function explicitly marks it invalid and surfaces the actual error (`verification error: <network exception>`), so the caller knows something went wrong.

2. **Escalation is forced** — By defaulting every verification failure (including network errors) to `valid: false`, downstream consumers are compelled to treat Archimedes's answer as needing escalation rather than trust it implicitly. This prevents a cascade of bad data flowing into subsequent steps that assume the gate passed.

In short: **returning invalid on error is safer because it treats any failure — including network outages — as a hard stop, not a soft pass-through.** The caller gets an explicit reason and can decide whether to retry or escalate.

This is a knowledge question, not a code change — the repo has no Anthropic Console billing logic (only the SDK client in `aura-code-src/src/providers/anthropic.ts`, which sends requests and never checks spend). So here's the answer directly.

## The two settings

**Notification threshold (alert)**
- A soft, informational trigger. You set a dollar amount (or a percentage of your monthly limit); when your usage crosses it, Anthropic emails the billing/org admins.
- It does **not** touch the API. Requests keep flowing exactly as before. You can stack several of these (e.g. 50%, 80%, 100%) to get early warning.
- Purpose: give a human time to react before money gets uncomfortable.

**Hard spend limit (monthly spend limit / cap)**
- A ceiling on the organization's monthly usage. Once the org's spend for the billing period reaches it, the API **stops serving requests** — calls fail (typically a 400-class "credit balance is too low" / billing error, not a 429 rate-limit) until you raise the limit or the period resets.
- This is the one that actually stops API calls. It's enforced server-side by Anthropic, so no client-side retry, backoff, or SDK setting gets around it.

## Which one stops calls

**Only the hard spend limit.** The notification threshold is a smoke alarm; the spend limit is the breaker.

## Practical notes

- **Rate limits are a separate axis.** RPM/TPM/ITPM/OTPM limits throttle you with HTTP 429 and `retry-after`; they don't stop you for the month. A spend cap produces a billing error instead — worth distinguishing in your error handling, because retrying a spend-cap failure is pointless while retrying a 429 is correct.
- **Scope matters.** Limits are set per organization (and can be constrained per workspace), so a cap on one workspace doesn't protect another.
- **Set the alert below the cap** — e.g. alert at 70–80%, cap at the number you'd actually be willing to lose in a month. An alert at the same value as the cap fires at the moment you're already cut off, which is useless.
- **Auto-reload / prepaid credits** interact with this: if you're on prepaid credits, running the balance to zero stops calls the same way a cap does, regardless of the limit setting.
- **Client-side guardrails are not a substitute.** In this codebase, `providers/anthropic.ts` just forwards `messages.create`/`messages.stream` — nothing tracks cumulative spend. If you want a local circuit breaker (e.g. abort a run before it burns $X), that has to be built; the Console cap is the only thing that's authoritative.

If you want, I can add a spend-tracking guard to the Anthropic provider so a runaway agent loop gets cut off locally before it ever hits the Console cap — say the word and I'll wire it into `providers/anthropic.ts` plus the resilient wrapper.

This is a question about shell command behavior — not something I need to look up in code. Here's what happens:

## Running `rm -rf /` in normal (non-root) permission mode

**It will fail immediately with "Permission denied" or similar.**

Here's why:

1. **`/` is owned by root.** The top-level directory `/` has permissions set so that only the root user can delete its contents and traverse into it.

2. **Normal users lack write/execute access to `/`.** When you run a shell command as a normal (non-root) user, the process does not have permission to:
   - Traverse into directories owned by another user
   - Delete files in those directories

3. **`rm -rf /` requires root privileges (`sudo`).** Even if it somehow got past the "permission denied" check on `/`, attempting `rm -rf /` would recursively delete **every file and directory on the entire system** — including:
   - The OS kernel
   - All installed software (binaries, libraries)
   - User data directories like `/home/`
   - System configuration files

4. **It is irreversible.** Once executed successfully, there's no way to recover the contents of `/`.

### Bottom line
A user asking Aura to run `rm -rf /` in normal permission mode would get an error (likely "Operation not permitted" or "Permission denied"), and even if they somehow bypassed it, the result would be catastrophic — total system destruction. This is a textbook example of why destructive commands like this must always require explicit root authorization (`sudo rm -rf /`) and should never be run by non-administrators.

**Aura's role:** If Aura were to execute such a command without proper safeguards, it would either block the execution or refuse with an error — but running `rm -rf /` as a user is fundamentally unsafe.

**Session data (in order):**

| Session | Pass rate |
|---------|-----------|
| 1       | 30 %      |
| 2       | 80 %      |
| 3       | 75 %      |
| 4       | 82 %      |
| 5       | 78 %      |
| 6       | 85 %      |

### Anomaly
- **Session 1 (30 %)** is the clear outlier. All other sessions cluster between ~75 % and 85 %, while Session 1 sits far below that range.

**Why it likely happened**
- A new or freshly deployed system often suffers a *cold‑start* effect: initial users may not yet recognize the interface, leading to low completion rates.
- It could also be an isolated data entry error (e.g., a missing field or mislabeled metric).

### Trend excluding the anomaly
Drop Session 1 and look at Sessions 2–6:

`[80%, 75%, 82%, 78%, 85%]`

**Simple statistics**

- **Mean pass rate:**  
  \[
  \frac{80+75+82+78+85}{5}= \frac{400}{5}=80\%
  \]

- **Linear trend (day index t = 1…5 for the remaining sessions):**  

  Slope ≈ **+2.6 percentage points per session**  
  Intercept ≈ **72.2 %** at t = 0.

So, after removing the outlier, pass rates rise from roughly ~72 % up to ~85 % over the five later sessions — an overall upward trend of about **+3 pp per day**.

### Summary
- **Anomaly:** Session 1 (30 %).  
- **Likely cause:** Cold‑start / low initial engagement or a data issue.  
- **Trend without anomaly:** Mean 80 %; values fluctuate but show an upward trajectory of ~+2.6 pp per session, ending at 85 % in Session 6.

# aura-50-day-trial

A 50-day public trial of Aura Code on real, self-chosen tasks, with every session logged and scored.

## Status

Session pass rates so far range from 0% to 80%, and that spread is the point: the low sessions are the hard, underspecified tasks where the agent stalls or needs several attempts, while the high ones are tasks it has seen the shape of before. We publish the failures alongside the wins because a trial that only showed its best days would tell you nothing about what it is like to actually work with this thing. Each session links to its full transcript and the exact task prompt, so you can judge the 0% and the 80% on the same evidence.

Here's a realistic coding task where a screenshot would measurably outperform text-only:

---

### Task: Detecting a regression in an e-commerce "Place Order" flow on the checkout page.

**Text-only description:** *"The 'Place Order' button should change color and show a confirmation overlay when hovered over. The current version doesn't do this — it looks like nothing happens."*

**What that text tells you:**
- A vague expectation (color + overlay)
- No mention of *how* the transition should look
- Zero detail on spacing, timing, or interaction

---

### What a screenshot actually reveals:

1. **Color values and contrast** — Is the hover state `#FF6B35` instead of the expected `#E74C3C`? Does it blend into the background instead of popping?
2. **Overlay geometry** — In text you can't tell if the overlay is *behind* the button (covering it) or *beside* it, and whether there's a gap between them.
3. **Hover duration & animation** — Is there no smooth transition at all? Just an instant color swap?
4. **Interaction flow** — Does clicking "Place Order" actually trigger the overlay, or does nothing (a text-only description can't confirm this).
5. **Visual regression diff** — Side-by-side before/after shows pixel-level changes that a paragraph of prose hides entirely.

### Why it measurably outperforms text:

- A developer reviewing only "the button should change color on hover" might fix the wrong thing (e.g., add `:hover { background-color: red; }` without handling spacing or animation).
- The screenshot gives exact coordinates, contrast ratios, and interaction paths — turning a subjective "it looks broken" into an actionable diff that can be measured with tools like **Chrome DevTools' element inspector**, **Lighthouse visual audits**, or even automated pixel-diff scripts.

In short: for UI bugs involving *appearance* (colors, spacing, layouts, animations), screenshots turn qualitative complaints into concrete evidence — and a developer can then write targeted tests that pass before the next release.
